Well-posedness for rough solutions of the 3D compressible Euler equations
Lars Andersson, Huali Zhang

Abstract
. In this paper we prove full local well-posedness for the Cauchy problem for the compressible 3D Euler equation, i.e. local existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence on initial data, with initial velocity, density and vorticity ( 𝐯 0 , ρ 0 , 𝐰 0 ) ∈ H 2 + × H 2 + × H 2 (\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{2} , improving on the regularity conditions of [ 63 ] . The continuous dependence on initial data for rough solutions of the compressible Euler system is new, even with the same regularity conditions as in [ 63 ] . In addition, we prove new local well-posedness results for the 3D compressible Euler system with entropy.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35Q35, 35R05, 35L60, 76N10

1.
Introduction and Results

We consider the Cauchy problem for the
compressible Euler equations (cf.
[
37
]
) in three dimensions,

\begin{cases}\varrho_{t}+\text{div}\left(\varrho\mathbf{v}\right)=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
\mathbf{v}_{t}+\left(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\right)\mathbf{v}+\frac{1}{\varrho}\nabla p=0,\\
\partial_{t}h+\left(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\right)h=0,\end{cases}

(1.1)

with a state function given by

p=p(\varrho,h)=\varrho^{\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{h}\quad(\gamma>1\ \mathrm{or}\ \gamma=-1)

(1.2)

and initial data

(\varrho,\mathbf{v},h)|_{t=0}=(\varrho_{0},\mathbf{v}_{0},h_{0}),

(1.3)

where
\mathbf{v}=(v^{1},v^{2},v^{3})^{\text{T}},\varrho
,
h
, and
p
respectively, denote the fluid velocity, density, entropy and pressure,
v^{1},v^{2},v^{3},\varrho,h
and
p
are scalar functions denoted from
(t,\mathbf{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The compressible Euler equation
describes the motion of an ideal fluid. A fluid with constant entropy
h
is called isentropic. The phenomena displayed in the interior of a fluid fall into two broad classes, the phenomena of waves, and
of vortex motion.

In order to display the wave and vortex structure in the Euler equation, and to fix notations,
we state the following definitions and lemmas, which can also be found in
[
36
,
50
]
.

1.1.
Reductions and definitions

Let us first introduce some variables—logarithmic density, specific vorticity and the acoustic metric.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\bar{\rho}>0
be a constant background density. The logarithmic density
{\rho}
and specific vorticity
\mathbf{w}
are defined by

\rho:=\ln\left(\frac{\varrho}{{\bar{\rho}}}\right),

(1.4)

\mathbf{w}:={\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}}{\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{v}},

(1.5)

and we write
\mathbf{w}=(w^{1},w^{2},w^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
{\rho}
be the logarithmic density. We define the vector
\mathbf{H}
as

\mathbf{H}:=\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\nabla h.

(1.6)

Let
\mathbf{w}
be defined in (
1.5
).

Definition 1.3
.

We denote the speed of sound
1
1

1

If
h\neq\mathrm{constant}
, then
c_{s}
is determined by
\rho,\mathbf{v}
and
h
. If
h=\mathrm{constant}
,
c_{s}
only depends on
\rho
and
\mathbf{v}
.

c_{s}:=\sqrt{\frac{\text{d}p}{\text{d}\varrho}}.

(1.7)

Based on above definitions, let us introduce a reduction of (
1.1
) under the above variables.

Lemma 1.1
.

[
36
]

Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)
be a solution of (
1.1
). Let
\mathbf{w}
and
\rho
are defined in (
1.4
)-(
1.5
). The 3D compressible Euler equations (
1.1
) can be written in the form

\begin{cases}\mathbf{T}v^{i}=-c^{2}_{s}\partial^{i}\rho-\frac{1}{\gamma}c^{2}_{s}\partial^{i}h,\\
\mathbf{T}\rho=-\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v},\\
\mathbf{T}h=0,\end{cases}

(1.8)

where

\mathbf{T}:=\partial_{t}+\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla.

(1.9)

We now introduce the acoustic metric, which will be used to reduce (
1.8
) to a wave-transport system.

Definition 1.4
.

We define the acoustic metric
g
and the inverse acoustic metric
g^{-1}
relative to the Cartesian coordinates as follows:

\begin{split}&g:=-dt\otimes dt+c_{s}^{-2}\sum_{a=1}^{3}\left(dx^{a}-v^{a}dt\right)\otimes\left(dx^{a}-v^{a}dt\right),\\
&g^{-1}:=-(\partial_{t}+v^{a}\partial_{a})\otimes(\partial_{t}+v^{b}\partial_{b})+c_{s}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\partial_{i}\otimes\partial_{i}.\end{split}

(1.10)

Following
[
63
]
, we introduce a decomposition for the velocity

v^{i}=v_{+}^{i}+v_{-}^{i},

(1.11)

where the vector
\mathbf{v}_{-}=(v_{-}^{1},v_{-}^{2},v_{-}^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
is determined by

-\Delta v_{-}^{i}:=\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}.

(1.12)

Lemma 1.2
.

[
50
]

Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)
be a solution of (
1.8
). Let
{\rho}
and
\mathbf{w}
be given by (
1.4
)-(
1.5
). Then the system (
1.8
) can be written as

\begin{cases}\square_{g}v^{i}=-\mathrm{e}^{\rho}c_{s}^{2}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}+Q^{i},\\
\square_{g}{\rho}=-\frac{1}{\gamma}c_{s}^{2}\Delta h+D,\\
\square_{g}h=c_{s}^{2}\Delta h+E,\\
\mathbf{T}h=0,\\
\mathbf{T}w^{i}=w^{a}\partial_{a}v^{i}+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\epsilon^{iab}\partial_{a}\rho\partial_{b}h,\end{cases}

(1.13)

where
Q^{i}
,
D
and
E
are quadratic forms, given by

\begin{split}{Q^{i}}:=&-\frac{2}{\gamma}e^{\rho}c^{2}_{s}\epsilon^{i}_{ab}w^{b}\partial^{a}h-(1+c_{s}^{-1}\frac{\partial c_{s}}{\partial\rho})g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}{\rho}\partial_{\beta}v^{i}-\frac{1}{\gamma}c^{2}_{s}\epsilon^{iab}w_{a}\partial_{b}h\\
&-\frac{1}{2}c^{2}_{s}\partial^{a}h\partial_{a}v^{i}+\frac{2c_{s}}{\gamma}\frac{\partial c_{s}}{\partial\rho}\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}\partial^{i}h+c^{3}_{s}\partial_{\beta}v^{i}\partial_{\alpha}(c^{-3}_{s}g^{\alpha\beta}),\\
{D:}=&-3c_{s}^{-1}\frac{\partial c_{s}}{\partial\rho}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}\rho+2\textstyle{\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq 3}}\big\{\partial_{a}v^{a}\partial_{b}v^{b}-\partial_{a}v^{b}\partial_{b}v^{a}\big\}\\
&-\frac{1}{\gamma}c^{2}_{s}\partial^{a}h\partial_{a}\rho-\frac{1}{\gamma}c^{2}_{s}\partial^{a}h\partial_{a}h+c^{3}_{s}\partial_{\beta}\rho\partial_{\alpha}(c^{-3}_{s}g^{\alpha\beta}),\\
E:=&c^{3}_{s}\partial_{\beta}h\partial_{\alpha}(c^{-3}_{s}g^{\alpha\beta}),\end{split}

(1.14)

and where the operator
\square_{g}
is given by

\square_{g}:=g^{\alpha\beta}\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}=-\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}+c^{2}_{s}\Delta,\quad\Delta=\partial^{2}_{1}+\partial^{2}_{2}+\partial^{2}_{3},

(1.15)

with
\mathbf{T}
as in (
1.9
).

For brevity, we set
\mathbf{Q}=(Q^{1},Q^{2},Q^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
.

Remark 1.1
.

We can also write
Q^{i}=Q^{i\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}h+Q^{i\alpha\beta}_{1j}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}v^{j}+Q^{i\alpha\beta}_{2j}\partial_{\alpha}h\partial_{\beta}v^{j}
,
D=D^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}h+D^{\alpha\beta}_{1j}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}v^{j}+D^{\alpha\beta}_{2j}\partial_{\alpha}h\partial_{\beta}v^{j}
, and
E=E^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}h+E^{\alpha\beta}_{1j}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}v^{j}+E^{\alpha\beta}_{2j}\partial_{\alpha}h\partial_{\beta}v^{j}
. Here
Q^{i\alpha\beta}
,
Q^{i\alpha\beta}_{1j}
,
Q^{i\alpha\beta}_{2j}
,
D^{\alpha\beta}
,
D^{\alpha\beta}_{1j}
,
D^{\alpha\beta}_{2j}
,
E^{\alpha\beta}
,
E^{\alpha\beta}_{1j}
,
E^{\alpha\beta}_{2j}
are smooth functions of
\mathbf{v}
,
\rho
and
h
, and they are uniquely determined by (
1.14
).

Remark 1.2
.

The system (
1.13
) is derived from (
1.1
), with (
1.4
)-(
1.5
), and using this convention, the systems (
1.1
), (
1.8
), and (
1.13
), as well as (
2.1
) below, are equivalent.

Remark 1.3
.

Using (
1.13
), (
1.11
) and (
1.12
), we have

\begin{split}&\square_{g}v^{i}_{+}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}v_{-}^{i}+Q^{i}.\end{split}

(1.16)

This will be proved in Lemma
2.3
below.

Lemma 1.3
.

Taking
h=
constant in (
1.8
), we then have

\begin{cases}&\mathbf{T}v^{i}=-c^{2}_{s}\partial^{i}\rho,\\
&\mathbf{T}\rho=-\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v},\end{cases}

(1.17)

Taking
h=
constant in System (
1.13
), then the system (
1.13
) is reduced to

\begin{cases}\square_{g}v^{i}=-\mathrm{e}^{\rho}c_{s}^{2}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}+\mathcal{Q}^{i},\\
\square_{g}{\rho}=\mathcal{D},\\
\mathbf{T}w^{i}=w^{a}\partial_{a}v^{i}.\end{cases}

(1.18)

Above,
\mathcal{Q}^{i}
and
\mathcal{D}
are quadratic null forms, which are defined by

\begin{split}{\mathcal{Q}^{i}}:=&-(1+c_{s}^{-1}c^{\prime}_{s})g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}{\rho}\partial_{\beta}v^{i}+c^{3}_{s}\partial_{\beta}v^{i}\partial_{\alpha}(c^{-3}_{s}g^{\alpha\beta}),\\
{\mathcal{D}:}=&-3c_{s}^{-1}c^{\prime}_{s}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}\rho+2\textstyle{\sum_{1\leq a<b\leq 3}}\big\{\partial_{a}v^{a}\partial_{b}v^{b}-\partial_{a}v^{b}\partial_{b}v^{a}\big\}+c^{3}_{s}\partial_{\beta}\rho\partial_{\alpha}(c^{-3}_{s}g^{\alpha\beta}).\end{split}

(1.19)

Remark 1.4
.

We can also write
\mathcal{Q}^{i}=\mathcal{Q}^{i\alpha\beta}_{j}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}v^{j}+
,
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\alpha\beta}_{j}\partial_{\alpha}\rho\partial_{\beta}v^{j}
. Here
\mathcal{Q}^{i\alpha\beta}_{j}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\alpha\beta}_{j}
are smooth functions of
\mathbf{v}
,
\rho
, and they are uniquely determined by (
1.19
).

Remark 1.5
.

The system (
1.18
) can also be derived from (
1.17
) under (
1.4
).

1.2.
Previous results

Let us recall the historical results starting from the incompressible fluid. For the Cauchy problem of
n
-dimensional incompressible Euler equations:

\begin{cases}\mathbf{v}_{t}+\left(\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla\right)\mathbf{v}+\nabla p=0,\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}=0,\\
\mathbf{v}|_{t=0}=\mathbf{v}_{0},\end{cases}

(1.20)

Kato and Ponce in
[
25
]
proved the local well-posedness of (
1.20
) if
\mathbf{v}_{0}\in W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),s>1+\frac{n}{p}
. Chae in
[
13
]
proved the local existence of solutions by setting
\mathbf{v}_{0}
in Triebel-Lizorkin spaces. In the opposite direction, Bourgain and Li
[
11
,
12
]
proved that the Cauchy problem is ill-posed for
\mathbf{v}_{0}\in W^{1+\frac{n}{p}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),1\leq p<\infty,n=2,3
. Very recently, Andersson and Kapitanski
[
6
]
proved the well-posedness of low regularity solutions of incompressible Neo-Hookean materials for
s>\frac{7}{4}(n=2)
or
s>2(n=3)
in Lagrangian coordinates, with some additional regularity conditions on the vorticity.

In the irrotational and isentropic case, the compressible Euler equations can be reduced to a system of quasilinear wave equations (see Lemma
1.2
and taking
\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{v}=0
). Consider a the Cauchy problem for a quasilinear wave equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{h(\phi)}\phi=q(d\phi,d\phi),\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
&\phi|_{t=0}=\phi_{0},\partial_{t}\phi|_{t=0}=\phi_{1},\end{cases}

(1.21)

where
\phi
is a scalar function,
h(\phi)
is a Lorentzian metric depending on
\phi
,
d=(\partial_{t},\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\cdots,\partial_{n})
, and
q
is quadratic in
d\phi
. Set the initial data
(\phi_{0},\phi_{1})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By using classical energy methods, Hughes-Kato-Marsden in
[
19
]
proved the local well-posedness of the problem (
1.21
) for
s>\frac{n}{2}+1
. On the other hand, Lindblad in
[
34
]
constructed some counterexamples for (
1.21
) when
s=2,n=3
. For
s={\frac{7}{4}},n=2
, a 2D counterexample was constructed by Ohlman
[
57
]
. There is a gap between the results
[
19
]
and
[
34
,
57
]
. To lower the regularity required for well-posedness, one may use estimates of Strichartz type, that provide integrated space-time estimates for
d\phi
. Work on Strichartz estimates has evolved from linear analysis, to the study of bilinear wave intersections, and next to the study of nonlinear wave intersections. The first stage is to study the Strichartz estimates of a wave equation with variable coefficients

\square_{h(t,x)}\phi=0,

(1.22)

and then exploit it to obtain the low regularity solutions of (
1.21
).

Kapitanskii
[
24
]
and Mockenhaupt-Seeger-Sogge
[
39
]
proved Strichartz estimates of (
1.22
) with smooth coefficients
h
. For a rough metric
h\in C^{2}
, the study of Strichartz estimates of (
1.22
) in two or three dimensions began with Smith’s result
[
45
]
. This was extended to all dimensions by Tataru
[
55
]
. In the opposite direction, Smith and Sogge
[
46
]
showed that the Strichartz estimates fail for
h\in C^{\alpha},\alpha<2
. The first improvement in study of nonlinear wave equations was independently achieved by Bahouri-Chemin
[
8
]
and Tataru
[
54
]
, who proved the local well-posedness of (
1.21
) with
s>\frac{n}{2}+\frac{7}{8},n=2
or
s>\frac{n}{2}+\frac{3}{4},n\geq 3
. This corresponding 3D result was also obtained by Klainerman in
[
26
]
using the vector-field approach. Shortly afterward, Tataru
[
56
]
relaxed the Sobolev indices to
s>\frac{n+1}{2}+\frac{1}{6},n\geq 3
. Also, Smith-Tataru
[
48
]
showed that the
\frac{1}{6}
loss is sharp for variable coefficients. Thus, to improve the above results of (
1.21
), one needs to exploit a new way or structure of (
1.21
).

By exploiting additional geometrical structure of the metric and introducing a decomposition of curvature, the 3D result was improved by Klainerman and Rodnianski
[
29
]
, with regularity exponent satisfying
s>2+\frac{2-\sqrt{3}}{2}
. Inspired by
[
29
]
, Geba in
[
22
]
studied the local existence and uniqueness of 2D quasilinear wave equations for
s>\frac{7}{4}+\frac{5-\sqrt{22}}{4}
. In 2005, a sharp result was established by Smith and Tataru in
[
47
]
, and they proved the local existence and uniqueness of solution of (
1.21
) if the regularity of initial data satisfies
s>\frac{7}{4},n=2
or
s>2,n=3
or
s>\frac{n+1}{2},4\leq n\leq 5
. An alternative proof of the 3D result in
[
47
]
was also obtained by Q. Wang
[
62
]
using the vector-field approach .

We should also mention the important progress in Einstein vacuum equations. In Yang-Mills gauge, the
L^{2}
conjecture is solved by Klainerman-Rodnianski-Szeftel
[
31
]
. In wave gauge, the system is well-posed in
H^{2+}
and ill-posed in
H^{2}
, cf. Klainerman-Rodnianski
[
30
]
and Ettinger-Lindblad
[
17
]
. For the time-like minimal surface equations, which satisfy null conditions, Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
1
]
obtained a substantial improvement, namely by
\frac{1}{8}
derivatives in two space dimensions and by
\frac{1}{4}
derivatives in higher dimensions. For other local well-posedness results of (
1.21
) in various settings, we refer the readers to
[
2
,
5
,
21
,
51
,
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
65
,
70
,
69
]
.

For the compressible Euler system, local well-posedness of (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) was initially obtained by Majda
[
37
]
, with
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\varrho_{0},h_{0})\in H^{s},s>1+\frac{n}{2}
and the density bounded away from vacuum. Following this result, there is a huge literature that deals with blow up problems, the incompressible limit, and free boundary problems, cf.
[
14
,
15
,
18
,
20
,
27
,
28
,
35
,
36
,
40
,
41
,
44
,
49
]
and reference therein. In the last several years, much progress has been made on low regularity problems of (
1.1
)-(
1.3
). The first improvement of rough solutions was independently obtained by Q. Wang
[
63
]
and Disconzi-Luo-Mazzone-Speck
[
16
]
. Q. Wang
[
63
]
established the existence and uniqueness of solutions of (
1.18
) when
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\ 2<s_{0}<s
. Disconzi-Luo-Mazzone-Speck
[
16
]
proved the existence and uniqueness of solutions of (
1.13
) if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0},h_{0})\in H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{3+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with an additional Hölder condition on the initial data for
\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}
. In the reverse direction, An-Chen-Yin
[
3
]
proved ill-posedness of (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) for initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0})\in\dot{H}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with smooth
\mathbf{w}_{0}
. In two dimensions, Zhang
[
67
,
68
]
proved the existence, uniqueness and continuous dependence of solutions of 2D isentropic compressible Euler equations if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\partial\mathbf{w}_{0}\in L^{\infty}
or
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{\frac{7}{4}+}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Recently, An-Chen-Yin
[
4
]
showed that the Cauchy problem for 2D compressible Euler equations is ill-posed if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0})\in\dot{H}^{\frac{7}{4}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

In this paper, we improve on the above mentioned results on the Cauchy problem for the compressible Euler system in several different ways. In particular, we prove local well-posedness including continuous dependence on initial data, for data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0})\in H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, improving on the result of Q. Wang. We also prove local well-posedness for data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{5/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{5/2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{5/2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{3/2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
.

1.3.
Motivation

Let us go back to the 3D results
[
63
]
and
[
16
]
. In
[
63
,
16
]
, to establish Strichartz estimates of the velocity and density, the regularity of the vorticity is assumed to be strictly greater than
2
. This turns out to be a very crucial condition, due to the role played in the Strichartz estimates of the regularity of the acoustic metric, and therefore of the vorticity.
As discussed in
[
63
,
16
]
, both the vector field approach and the method of Smith and Tataru fail to establish Strichartz estimates when the Sobolev regularity of the vorticity and the corresponding foliations of space-time is less than or equal to
2
.
It is known that the classical Strichartz estimates fail to hold for spacetime metrics with regularity
C^{\alpha}
,
\alpha<2
, cf.
[
46
]
. See also
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
for estimates of Strichartz type under weaker regularity assumptions.

In view of the just mentioned facts, well-posedness for (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) with
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{2+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{3+}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{s^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\ s^{\prime}\leq 2
, remains a challenging open problem, since the regularity of foliations will be less than or equal to
2
. However, further considering the role of the term
\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}
in the Strichartz estimates leads to new insights.

Let us consider a simplified model of (
1.18
). We also make note of the better variable
\mathbf{v}_{+}
in (
1.16
). Thus, let
\mathbf{V}
be a vector function satisfying the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{m}}\mathbf{V}=-\text{curl}\mathbf{W},\quad(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{V}_{+}+\mathbf{V}_{-},\quad-\Delta\mathbf{V}_{-}=\text{curl}\mathbf{W},\quad\mathbf{W}=\text{curl}\mathbf{V},\\
&\square_{\mathbf{m}}\mathbf{V}_{+}=\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\mathbf{V}_{-}),\\
&\partial_{t}\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{W}\cdot\partial\mathbf{V},\\
&\partial_{t}\text{curl}\text{curl}\mathbf{W}=\text{curl}\text{curl}\mathbf{W}\cdot\partial\mathbf{V},\end{cases}

(1.23)

with initial data

(\mathbf{V},\partial_{t}\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W})_{t=0}=(\mathbf{V}_{0},\mathbf{V}_{1},\mathbf{W}_{0})\in H_{x}^{2+}\times H_{x}^{1+}\times H_{x}^{2}.

Above,
\mathbf{m}
is a standard Minkovski metric. If
\mathbf{W}_{0}\in H_{x}^{2}
, one would at most hope that
\text{curl}\mathbf{W}\in H_{x}^{1}
. So we cannot obtain the Strichartz estimates of
\|d\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}
directly via the first equation
\square_{\mathbf{m}}\mathbf{V}=-\text{curl}\mathbf{W}
in (
1.23
). Fortunately, by using Duhamel’s principle, Strichartz estimates for
\square_{\mathbf{m}}f=0
(cf.
[
53
]
), and Sobolev’s imbeddings for
d\mathbf{v}_{-}
, it is possible to prove that there is a Strichartz estimate for
d\mathbf{V}
and that (
1.23
) is locally well-posed. Moreover, there exists a finite time-interval
[0,t]
such that

\|d\mathbf{V}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,t]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\|\mathbf{V}_{0}\|_{H^{2+}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{V}_{1}\|_{H^{1+}_{x}}+\|\mathbf{W}_{0}\|_{H^{2}_{x}}.

(1.24)

The wave-transport system (
1.18
) coupled with (
1.16
) is very similar to (
1.23
) if we replace
\mathbf{m}
by a Lorentz metric depending on
\mathbf{V}
. Using finite speed of propagation and restricting to a small time interval,
we shall reduce (
1.18
) to small data problem. Then the acoustic metric
g
can be viewed as a perturbation of
\mathbf{m}
. So we may expect there exists a local solution of (
1.18
) for
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{2+}\times H^{2+}\times H^{2}
or
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{\frac{5}{2}}\times H^{\frac{5}{2}}\times H^{\frac{3}{2}+}
. Our approach differs from that of
[
63
]
and
[
16
]
, for they prove the Strichartz estimates of solutions of (
1.18
) by reducing the problem to conformal energy estimates of linear wave equations, whereas our method is to reduce to a small data problem, and then prove the Strichartz estimates of linear wave equations endowed with the acoustic metric
g
by constructing approximate solutions.

On the other hand, for the time-like minimal surface equations, a quasilinear model, the work of Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
1
]
shows that a significant improvement can be achieved by exploiting the structure of Strichartz estimates and
of equations. Ai-Ifrim-Tataru were able to lower the regularity requirements for well-posedness by
\frac{1}{4}
derivatives in three spatial dimensions compared to the sharp results of Smith-Tataru for the generic case (
1.21
). Indeed, in our former preprint
[
66
]
, in the reduction of small solutions, if
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in H^{2+}
, we found that there is a Strichartz estimate of solutions of (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) only depending on the quantities
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{H_{x}^{2+}}
,
\|\rho\|_{H_{x}^{2+}}
,
\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H_{x}^{3/2+}}
, and
\|h\|_{H_{x}^{5/2+}}
. This allows us to study Strichartz estimates and low regularity solutions of (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) when
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in H^{s^{\prime}}(s^{\prime}\leq 2)
based on the pioneering works
[
63
,
16
]
, and our former preprint
[
66
]
.

1.4.
Statement of results

Before stating our main theorem, we introduce some notation and function spaces.
Let
\partial=(\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\partial_{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
,
d=(\partial_{t},\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\partial_{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
,
\left<\xi\right>=(1+|\xi|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}},\ \xi\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Denote by
\left<\partial\right>
the corresponding Bessel potential multiplier. Denote the fractional operator
\Lambda_{x}=(-\Delta)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The symbol
\epsilon^{ijk}(i,j,k=1,2,3)
denotes the standard volume form on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Let
{\Delta}_{j}
be the homogeneous frequency localized operator with frequency
2^{j},j\in\mathbb{Z}
, cf.
[
9
]
.

For
f\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we let

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{s}}:=\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}+\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})},

(1.25)

with the homogeneous norm
\|f\|^{2}_{\dot{H}^{s}}:={\sum_{j\geq-1}}2^{2js}\|{\Delta}_{j}f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}
. We shall also make use of the homogenous Besov norm

\displaystyle\|f\|^{r}_{\dot{B}^{s}_{p,r}}:={\sum_{j\geq-1}}2^{jsr}\|{\Delta}_{j}f\|^{r}_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}.

(1.26)

To avoid confusion, when the function
f
is related to both time and space variables, we use the notation
\|f\|_{H_{x}^{s}}:=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}
.

Assume there holds

|\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0}|\leq C_{0},\qquad c_{s}|_{t=0}>c_{0}>0,

(1.27)

where
C_{0},c_{0}>0
are constants. In the following, constants
C
depending only on
C_{0},c_{0}
shall be called universal. Unless otherwise stated, all constants that appear are universal in this sense.
The notation
X\lesssim Y
means
X\leq CY
, where
C
is a universal constant, possibly depending on
C_{0},c_{0}
. Similarly, we write
X\simeq Y
when
C_{1}Y\leq X\leq C_{2}Y
, with
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
universal constants, and
X\ll Y
when
X\leq CY
for a sufficiently large constant
C
.

Let
s_{0},s
satisfy
2<s_{0}<s\leq\frac{5}{2}
.
We also set

\begin{split}&\delta_{0}=s_{0}-2,\quad\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}),\\
&\delta_{1}=\frac{s-2}{10}.\end{split}

(1.28)

We use four small parameters

0<\epsilon_{3}\ll\epsilon_{2}\ll\epsilon_{1}\ll\epsilon_{0}\ll 1.

(1.29)

We are now ready to state the main result of this paper.

Theorem 1.4
.

Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)-(
1.3
). Let
\rho
and
\mathbf{w}
be defined in (
1.4
)-(
1.5
).
Let
2<s_{0}<s\leq\frac{5}{2}
. For given initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
satisfying (
1.27
) and for any
M_{0}>0
such that

\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}}}+\|h_{0}\|_{H^{1+s_{0}}}\leq M_{0},

(1.30)

there exist positive constants
T>0

and
M_{1}>0
(
T
and
{M}_{1}
depends on
C_{0},c_{0},s,s_{0},{M}_{0}
) such that (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) has a unique solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho,\mathbf{w},h)
satisfying

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v},\rho)\in C([0,T],H_{x}^{s})\cap C^{1}([0,T],H_{x}^{s-1}),

(1.31)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}\in C([0,T],H_{x}^{s_{0}})\cap C^{1}([0,T],H_{x}^{s_{0}-1}),

(1.32)

\displaystyle h\in C([0,T],H_{x}^{1+s_{0}})\cap C^{1}([0,T],H_{x}^{s_{0}}).

(1.33)

The following statements hold.

(1)

The solutions
\mathbf{v},\rho,h
and
\mathbf{w}
satisfy the energy estimate

\begin{split}&\|(\mathbf{v},\rho)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s_{0}}}+\|h\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{1+s_{0}}}\leq M_{1},\\
&\|(\partial_{t}\mathbf{v},\partial_{t}\rho)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s-1}}+\|\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s_{0}-1}}+\|\partial_{t}h\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s_{0}}}\leq M_{1},\end{split}

and

\|\mathbf{v},\rho,h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,{T}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\leq 1+C_{0}.

(2)

The solution
\mathbf{v},\rho,h
and
\mathbf{v}_{+}
satisfy the Strichartz estimate

\|(d\mathbf{v},d\rho,d\mathbf{v}_{+})\|_{L^{2}_{t}L_{x}^{\infty}}+\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho,dh\|_{L^{2}_{t}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}+\|\partial\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{2}_{t}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\leq M_{1}.

(1.34)

(3)

For any
1\leq r\leq s+1
, and for each
t_{0}\in[0,T)
, the linear equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}f=0,\qquad(t,x)\in(t_{0},T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{0}\in H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\partial_{t}f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{1}\in H^{r-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\end{cases}

(1.35)

admits a solution
f\in C([-T,T],H_{x}^{r})\times C^{1}([-T,T],H_{x}^{r-1})
and the following estimates hold:

\|f\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r}}+\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H_{x}^{r-1}}\leq C_{M_{0}}(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}).

Additionally, the following estimates hold, provided
k<r-1
,

\|\left<\partial\right>^{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C(\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}),

(1.36)

and the same estimates hold with
\left<\partial\right>^{k}
replaced by
\left<\partial\right>^{k-1}d
. Here,
C_{M_{0}}
is a constant depending on
C_{0},c_{0},s,s_{0}
and
M_{0}
.

(4)

the map from
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{s_{0}+1}\times H^{s_{0}}
to
(\mathbf{v},\rho,h,\mathbf{w})(t,\cdot)\in C([0,T];H_{x}^{s}\times H_{x}^{s}\times H_{x}^{s_{0}+1}\times H_{x}^{s_{0}})
is continuous.

Remark 1.6
.

(1)

Theorem
1.4
provides the first proof of strong well-posedness for the compressible Euler system with rough data.

(2)

Points (
1
), (
2
) of Theorem
1.4
recovers the result of Q. Wang
[
63
, Theorem 1.1]
.
In addition, Point (
3
) of Theorem
1.4
above
provides a Strichartz estimate (
1.36
) for a linear wave equation (
1.35
) endowed with the acoustic metric, and Point (
4
) gives the continuous dependence of the solution on the initial data, which is lacking in
[
63
]
.

(3)

We emphasize that the estimate (
1.36
) is much stronger than (
1.34
), and that the estimate (
1.36
) is a key observation to prove the continuous dependence of solutions, as well as Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.6
.

Remark 1.7
.

(1)

To ensure that (
1.1
) is hyperbolic in time, we assume that (
1.27
) holds. Equation (
1.35
) is devised for adapting to the structure of the acoustic metric.

(2)

The regularity exponent of the vorticity
s_{0}>2
is essential to constructing wave-packets, which implies a sufficient regularity of characteristic hypersurfaces. See Corollary
6.12
and Section
8
for details.

Our second result is as follows.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{9}
. Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)-(
1.3
). Let
\rho
and
\mathbf{w}
be defined in (
1.4
)-(
1.5
). For given initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
satisfying (
1.27
) and for any
\bar{M}_{0}>0
such that

\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}}+\|h_{0}\|_{H^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon}}\leq\bar{M}_{0},

(1.37)

there exist positive constants
\bar{T}>0
and
\bar{M}_{1}>0
(
\bar{T}
and
\bar{M}_{1}
depends on
C_{0},c_{0},\epsilon,\bar{M}_{0}
) such that (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) has a unique solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho)\in C([0,\bar{T}],H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}})
,
\mathbf{w}\in C([0,\bar{T}],H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon})
,
h\in C([0,\bar{T}],H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon})
. To be precise,

(1)

the solution
\mathbf{v},\rho,h
and
\mathbf{w}
satisfy the energy estimates

\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{v},\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}}+\|h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon}}+\|\mathbf{v},\rho,h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\leq\bar{M}_{1},\\
&\|\partial_{t}\mathbf{v},\partial_{t}\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}}}+\|\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}}+\|\partial_{t}h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}}\leq\bar{M}_{1},\end{split}

(2)

the solution
\mathbf{v},\rho
and
h
satisfy the Strichartz estimate

\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho,dh\|_{L^{2}_{[0,\bar{T}]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\leq\bar{M}_{1},

(3)

for any
\frac{7}{6}\leq r<\frac{7}{2}
, the linear wave equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}f=0,\\
&(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=0}=(f_{0},f_{1})\in H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\times H^{r-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\end{cases}

(1.38)

has a unique solution on
[0,\bar{T}]
. Moreover, for
a\leq r-\frac{7}{6}
, the solution satisfies

\begin{split}&\|\left<\partial\right>^{a-1}d{f}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,\bar{T}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq{\bar{M}_{3}}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r-1}}),\\
&\|{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H^{r-1}_{x}}\leq{\bar{M}_{3}}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}).\end{split}

(1.39)

Here
{\bar{M}_{3}}>0
depends on
C_{0},c_{0},\epsilon,\bar{M}_{0}
.

(4)

the map from
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{\frac{5}{2}}\times H^{\frac{5}{2}}\times H^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}\times H^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon}
to
(\mathbf{v},\rho,h,\mathbf{w})(t,\cdot)\in C([0,\bar{T}];H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}}\times H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}}\times H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}\times H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon})
is continuous.

Remark 1.8
.

By the work of Majda
[
37
]
, the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) is well-posed if
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0})\in H^{\frac{5}{2}+}
(which implies
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in H^{\frac{3}{2}+}
). Essentially, compared to the classical result
[
37
]
, Theorem
1.5

lowers the regularity requirement for the velocity and density from
H^{\frac{5}{2}+}
to
H^{\frac{5}{2}}
.

Remark 1.9
.

The main difficulty in proving Theorem
1.5

is that neither the vector-field approach in
[
63
]
nor the method of Smith-Tataru can be used directly if the vorticity has Sobolev’s regularity
s_{0}\leq 2
.

Remark 1.10
.

Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
hold if we replace
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0})
with
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0}-c_{1})
, with
c_{1}
is a constant in
\mathbb{R}
.

Our third result is about the isentropic compressible Euler equations.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
2<s<\frac{5}{2}
. Let
\rho
and
\mathbf{w}
be defined in (
1.4
)-(
1.5
). Consider the Cauchy problem (
1.17
) with the initial data

(\mathbf{v},\rho,\mathbf{w})|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0}).

(1.40)

Assume that (
1.27
) holds. For any
M_{*}>0
and initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
satisfying

\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{2}}\leq M_{*},

(1.41)

there exist positive constants
T^{*}>0
and
\bar{M}_{2}>0
(
T^{*}
and
\bar{M}_{2}
depends on
C_{0},c_{0},s,M_{*}
) such that (
1.17
)-(
1.40
) has a unique solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho)
satisfying
(\mathbf{v},\rho)\in C([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{s})\cap C^{1}([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{s-1})
,
\mathbf{w}\in C([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{2})\cap C^{1}([0,T^{*}],H_{x}^{1})
.
To be precise,

(1)

the solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho,\mathbf{w})
satisfies the energy estimate

\begin{split}&\|(\mathbf{v},\rho)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{2}}\leq\bar{M}_{2},\\
&\|(\partial_{t}\mathbf{v},\partial_{t}\rho)\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s-1}}+\|\partial_{t}\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{1}}\leq\bar{M}_{2},\end{split}

and

\|\mathbf{v},\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,{T}^{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}}\leq 2+C_{0}.

(2)

the solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho)

satisfies the Strichartz estimate

\|(d\mathbf{v},d\rho)\|_{L^{2}_{[0,{T}^{*}]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\leq\bar{M}_{2}.

(3)

for any
\frac{s}{2}\leq r\leq 3
, and for each
t_{0}\in[0,T^{*}]
, the linear wave equation (
1.38
) has a unique solution on
[0,T^{*}]
. Moreover, for
a\leq r-\frac{s}{2}
, the solution satisfies

\begin{split}&\|\left<\partial\right>^{a-1}d{f}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\bar{M}_{4}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r-1}}),\\
&\|{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H^{r-1}_{x}}\leq\bar{M}_{4}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}).\end{split}

(1.42)

Here
{\bar{M}_{4}}>0
depends on
C_{0},c_{0},s,{M}_{*}
.

(4)

the map from
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})\in H^{s}\times H^{s}\times H^{2}
to
(\mathbf{v},\rho,\mathbf{w})(t,\cdot)\in C([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{s}\times H_{x}^{s}\times H_{x}^{2})
is continuous.

Remark 1.11
.

Controlling the optimal regularity of velocity and density, some good structure of (
1.1
) may allow us lowering the regularity of the vorticity. In Theorem
1.6
, the regularity of vorticity is
2
. So it provides us
(s_{0}-2)
-order (
s_{0}>2
) regularity improvement of the vorticity compared with
[
63
]
.

Remark 1.12
.

Similar to Theorem
1.5
, the main difficulty in proving Theorem
1.6

is that neither the vector-field approach in
[
63
]
nor the method of Smith-Tataru can be used directly if the vorticity has regularity
s_{0}\leq 2
.

Remark 1.13
.

Comparing (
1.36
) with (
1.42
), (
1.39
), (
1.36
) is better than (
1.42
) and (
1.39
). There is a Strichartz estimate with a loss of derivatives in (
1.36
), which is proved by extending the short (semi-classical) time-interval to a regular time-interval by summing up estimates on these short time-intervals.

1.5.
Overview of the proofs

1.5.1.
A sketch of proof for Theorem
1.4

The main idea is as follows.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 1: energy estimates}
. The energy estimate relies on the combination of the hyperbolic structure and a transport structure of (
1.8
). Using the hyperbolic system, we have

\|(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)\|_{H_{x}^{a}}\leq\|(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0})\|_{H_{x}^{a}}\exp(C\int^{t}_{0}{\|(d\mathbf{v},d\rho,dh)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}}d\tau),\quad a\geq 0.

This implies that the energy estimates are independent of
vorticity. In fact, the key role of the vorticty is
in the Ricci curvature
R_{ll}
(here
l
is a null vector for the characteristic surfaces), not in the energy estimates, cf. Corollary
6.12
and Lemma
6.13
. Thus we also need to study the vorticity, especially with regard to its role in Strichartz estimates for velocity and density. Motivated by
[
63
,
36
]
, we derive some modified transport equations:

\mathbf{T}\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{v}+\partial\rho\partial h,

(1.43)

and

\begin{split}&\mathbf{T}(\mathrm{curl}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}-\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{k}h\Delta v_{j}+\partial\rho\partial\mathbf{w}+\partial\mathbf{v}\partial^{2}h)\\
=&\partial^{i}\big(2\partial_{n}v_{a}\partial^{n}w^{a}\big)+(\partial\mathbf{v},\partial\rho,\partial h)\cdot(\partial^{2}\rho,\partial\mathbf{v})\\
&+(\partial\mathbf{v},\partial\rho,\partial h)\cdot\partial^{3}h+(\partial\mathbf{v},\partial\rho,\partial h)\cdot\partial^{2}\mathbf{w}.\end{split}

(1.44)

We use the equation (
1.43
) to obtain an
L^{2}_{x}
estimate. To the estimate in the norm
\dot{H}^{s_{0}}_{x}
, we use the equation (
1.44
) to handle it. So the most difficult term is

\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\left\{\mathrm{curl}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}-\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{k}h\Delta v_{j}+\partial\rho\partial\mathbf{w}+\partial\mathbf{v}\partial^{2}h\right\}\cdot\partial^{i}\big(2\partial_{n}v_{a}\partial^{n}w^{a}\big)dxd\tau,

(1.45)

for the other terms can be estimated by Hölder equalities and commutator estimates. Integrating (
1.45
) by parts and using
\partial^{i}\mathrm{curl}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}=0
, so we have

\eqref{eq01}=\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\left\{\partial\rho\partial\mathbf{w}+\partial\mathbf{v}\partial^{2}h-\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{k}h\Delta v_{j}\right\}\cdot\partial^{i}\big(2\partial_{n}v_{a}\partial^{n}w^{a}\big)dxd\tau,

(1.46)

In contrast to
[
63
]
, we utilize the Plancherel formula to handle the following term

\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{i}\big(\partial_{n}v^{a}\partial^{n}w_{a}\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big(\partial{\rho}\partial\mathbf{w}\big)dxd\tau.

which after a partial integration becomes

\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{5}{2}}\partial_{i}\big(\partial_{n}v^{a}\partial^{n}\mathbf{w}_{a}\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{3}{2}}\big(\partial{\rho}\partial\mathbf{w}\big)dxd\tau.

Similarly, we can handle the second difficult term

\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{i}\big(\partial_{n}v^{a}\partial^{n}\mathbf{w}_{a}\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big(\partial{\mathbf{v}}\partial^{2}h\big)dxd\tau.

in the form

\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{5}{2}}\partial_{i}\big(\partial_{n}v^{a}\partial^{n}\mathbf{w}_{a}\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-\frac{3}{2}}\big(\partial{\mathbf{v}}\partial^{2}h\big)dxd\tau.

We also integrate the third difficult term by parts

\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{i}\big(\partial_{n}v^{a}\partial^{n}w_{a}\big)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big(\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{k}h\Delta v_{j}\big)dxd\tau.

(1.47)

Using
\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{i}\Delta v_{j}=\Delta\mathbf{w}^{k}
and
\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{i}\partial_{k}h=0
, we can transform (
1.47
) to

\begin{split}&\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big(\partial_{n}v^{a}\partial^{n}w_{a}\big)\cdot\{\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\{\partial_{i}(\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{k}h)\Delta v_{j}\}dx+\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}[\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\partial_{k}h\cdot\Delta\mathbf{w}^{k}]\}dxd\tau\\
=&\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\big(\partial_{n}v^{a}\partial^{n}w_{a}\big)\cdot\{\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\{\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{i}(\mathrm{e}^{-\rho})\partial_{k}h\Delta v_{j}\}dx+\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}[\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\partial_{k}h\cdot\Delta\mathbf{w}^{k}]\}dxd\tau.\end{split}

For the entropy, we
calculate

\begin{split}&\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{H}=\partial\rho\partial h,\\
&\mathbf{T}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})=\partial\mathbf{v}\partial^{2}h+\mathbf{w}\partial\rho\partial h,\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\mathbf{T}\left\{\partial^{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})\right\}=&-2\partial^{k}(\partial_{i}v^{j})\partial_{j}\partial^{i}h+\partial\mathbf{v}\partial^{3}h\\
&+\partial\mathbf{w}\partial\rho\partial h+\mathbf{w}\partial\rho\partial^{2}h\\
&+\mathbf{w}\partial\rho\partial\rho\partial h+\mathbf{w}\partial h\partial^{2}h.\end{split}

(1.48)

We can obtain an
L^{2}_{x}
estimate of
h
by using (
1.8
). To bound
\|h\|_{\dot{H}_{x}^{s_{0}+1}}
, we operate on (
1.48
) with
\Lambda^{s_{0}-2}_{x}
, multiply it with
\Lambda^{s_{0}-2}_{x}\partial^{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})
, and integrate over
[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The most difficult term is

\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda^{s_{0}-2}_{x}[\partial^{k}(\partial_{m}v^{j})\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h]\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau.

(1.49)

Integrating (
1.49
) by parts yields

\begin{split}&\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}[\partial^{k}v^{j}\partial_{m}(\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h)]\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau\\
&+\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}(\partial^{k}v^{j}\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau.\end{split}

(1.50)

The first term in (
1.50
) can be estimated by using product estimates. We rewrite the second term in (
1.50
) as

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}(\partial^{k}v^{j}\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\{(\partial^{k}v^{j}-\partial^{j}v^{k})\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h\}\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau

\displaystyle-\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}(\partial^{j}v^{k}\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\{\epsilon^{lkj}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}w_{l}\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h\}\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau

(1.51)

\displaystyle-\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}(\partial^{j}v^{k}\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau.

(1.52)

We then use the Plancherel formula and the Hölder inequality to bound (
1.51
). Integrating (
1.52
) by parts yields

\displaystyle\eqref{eq07}=

\displaystyle-2\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}(\partial^{j}v^{k}\partial_{k}\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{m}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau

(1.53)

\displaystyle-2\int^{t}_{0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}(\partial_{k}\partial^{j}v^{k}\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h)\cdot\Lambda_{x}^{s_{0}-2}\partial_{m}(\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{i}H^{i})dxd\tau.

(1.54)

By using Lemma
2.10
, we can bound (
1.53
). For (
1.54
), we use
\mathbf{T}\rho
to replace
\partial_{k}v^{k}
and then integrate it by parts. Gathering these above estimates together, and using Young’s inequality, Gronwall’s inequality, we can get the desired energy estimates:

\begin{split}&\|(\mathbf{v},\rho)\|_{H_{x}^{s}}+\|h\|_{H_{x}^{1+s_{0}}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}}}\leq E_{0}\exp\{{\int^{t}_{0}}(\|(d\mathbf{v},d\bm{\rho},dh)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\partial\mathbf{v},\partial h,\partial\rho\|_{\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}})d\tau\},\end{split}

(1.55)

where
E_{0}
are determined by the Sobolev norms
\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s}},\|\rho_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s}}
,
\|h_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}+1}}
, and
\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}}}
. Hence, a Strichartz estimate for

{\int^{t}_{0}}\|(d\mathbf{v},d\rho,dh)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}d\tau+{\int^{t}_{0}}\|\partial\mathbf{v},\partial\rho,\partial h\|_{\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}d\tau.

(1.56)

is needed.
See Section
3.1
for details.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 2: Reduction to a result with small initial data}
.
Since the system (
1.8
) has finite propagation speed, by a standard compactness method, scaling and localization technique, we can reduce Theorem
1.4
to the following statement. For any smooth, supported data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},{\rho}_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
satisfying

\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{v}_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}}}+\|h_{0}\|_{H^{s_{0}+1}}\leq\epsilon_{3},\end{split}

then the Cauchy problem (
1.8
) admits a smooth solution
(\mathbf{v},\rho,h,\mathbf{w})
on
[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which has the following properties:

\mathrm{(i)}
energy estimates

\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}}}+\|h\|_{H_{x}^{s_{0}+1}}\leq\epsilon_{2}.\end{split}

(1.57)

\mathrm{(ii)}
dispersive estimate for
\mathbf{v}
and
\rho

\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho,dh\|_{L^{2}_{t}C^{\delta}_{x}}+\|d\rho,\partial\mathbf{v}_{+},d\mathbf{v},dh\|_{L^{2}_{t}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\leq\epsilon_{2},

(1.58)

\mathrm{(iii)}
dispersive estimate for the linear equation

Let
f
satisfy
the equation

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=0,\qquad(t,x)\in[-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{0}\in H_{x}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\quad\partial_{t}f(t_{0},\cdot)=f_{1}\in H_{x}^{r-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}).\end{cases}

Here
\mathbf{g}
is defined in (
5.1
), which is a localization of
g
. For each
1\leq r\leq s+1
, the Cauchy problem (
1.35
) is well-posed in
H_{x}^{r}\times H_{x}^{r-1}
, and the following estimate holds:

\|\left<\partial\right>^{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{[-1,1]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H^{r-1}},\quad k<r-1.

(1.59)

See Section
4
for the complete proof.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 3: Reduction to a continuity argument}
. Due to the fact that the initial data
(\mathbf{v}_{0},{\rho}_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
is small, we can consider a perturbation problem around Minkovski space. We further
reduce the proof of (
1.57
)-(
1.59
) to: there is a continuous functional
G:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
, satisfying
G(0)=0
, so that for each
(\mathbf{v},\rho,h,\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{H}
satisfying
G(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)\leq 2\epsilon_{1}
the following hold:
\mathrm{(i)}
the function
\mathbf{v},\rho,h
, and
\mathbf{w}
satisfies
G(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)\leq\epsilon_{1}
;
\mathrm{(ii)}
the estimates (
1.57
)-(
1.58
) and (
1.59
) both hold. By using energy estimates in Theorem
3.2
, the estimate (
1.57
) follows from (
1.58
). Hence, the proof of
G(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)\leq\epsilon_{1}
and (
1.58
)-(
1.59
) are the core. See Section
5
for details.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 4: Definition of $G$ and characteristic energy estimates}
.

Following
[
47
]
, for
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and let
\Sigma_{\theta,r}
be the flowout of the set
\theta\cdot\mathbf{x}=r-2
along the null geodesic flow with respect of
\mathbf{g}
in the direction
\theta
at
t=-2
.
Let
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\theta}
be given orthonormal coordinates on the hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
perpendicular to
\theta
. Then,
\Sigma_{\theta,r}
is of the form

\Sigma_{\theta,r}=\left\{(t,\mathbf{x}):\theta\cdot\mathbf{x}-\phi_{\theta,r}=0\right\}

for a smooth function
\phi_{\theta,r}(t,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\theta})
.
For a given
\theta
, the family
\{\Sigma_{\theta,r},\ r\in\mathbb{R}\}
defines a foliation of
[-2,2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, by characteristic hypersurfaces with respect to
\mathbf{g}
.

We define (see (
6.2
) for details)

G=\sup_{\theta,r}|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|d\phi_{\theta,r}-dt|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}}.

(1.60)

For simplicity, let us suppose
\theta=(0,0,1)
and
r=0
. In this case, we set
\Sigma=\Sigma_{\theta,r}
and
\phi=\phi_{\theta,r}
. To prove
G\leq\epsilon_{1}
and catch the geometry properties of
\Sigma
, let us introduce a null frame along
\Sigma
as follows. Let

V=(dr)^{*},

where
r
is the foliation of
\Sigma
, and where
*
denotes the identification of covectors and vectors induced by
\mathbf{g}
. Define a null frame with respect to
\mathbf{g}
by setting

l=\left<dt,dx_{3}-d\phi\right>^{-1}_{\mathbf{g}}\left(dx_{3}-d\phi\right)^{*},\quad\underline{l}=l+2\partial_{t},

and choosing orthonormalized vector fields tangent to tangent to the fixed-time slice
\Sigma^{t}
of
\Sigma
.
By further calculation (see Corollary
6.6
, Lemma
6.8
, and Lemma
6.9
), the bound of
G
relies on

|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,{\Sigma}}
and
|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|(\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w},\Delta h)|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0}-1,2,{\Sigma}}
. Hence, we need go back to the hyperbolic structure and the wave-transport structure. By a change of coordinates, we could use the hyperbolic structure to get a bound of
|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|\mathbf{v},\rho,h|\kern-1.07639pt|\kern-1.07639pt|_{s_{0},2,{\Sigma_{\theta,r}}}
. As for
\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}
and
\Delta h
, the characteristic energy estimate is more difficult. For example, on the Cauchy slice
\{t=\tau\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we can use elliptic estimates to get the energy estimate of all derivatives of
\mathbf{w}
by using
\mathrm{div}\mathbf{w}
and
\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}
. However, on the characteristic hypersurface, this type of elliptic energy estimates does not work. To overcome this difficulty, we use the Hodge decomposition to recover some transport equations for derivatives of
\mathbf{w}
and
\Delta h
, which is related to Riesz operator. To do that, some commutator estimates for the Riesz operator and
\mathbf{v}\cdot\nabla
concerning the compressible fluid are required. See Lemma
2.16
,
2.15
,
2.18
,
6.7
, and
6.8
for details. For the whole proof of the characteristic energy estimates, cf. Section
6
.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 5: Strichartz estimates}
. We should note that (
1.59
) of linear wave is not sufficient for us to establish the desired Strichartz estimate (
1.58
). This leads us to introduce Lemma
2.14
. Using Lemma
2.14
, we are able to establish the following Strichartz estimate for a non-homogeneous linear wave equation. For any
1\leq r\leq s+1
, for each
t_{0}\in[-2,2)
, then the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}&\square_{\mathbf{g}}f=\mathbf{T}F,\qquad(t,x)\in(t_{0},2]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&f(t,x)|_{t=t_{0}}=f_{0}\in H_{x}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\\
&\partial_{t}f(t,x)|_{t=t_{0}}=f_{1}-F(t_{0},\cdot)\in H_{x}^{r-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),\end{cases}

(1.61)

admites a solution
f\in C([-2,2],H_{x}^{r})\times C^{1}([-2,2],H_{x}^{r-1})
and the following estimates holds, provided
k<r-1
,

\|\left<\partial\right>^{k}f,\left<\partial\right>^{k-1}df\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\|f_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|f_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}+\|F\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{r}}.

(1.62)

So (
1.62
) is stronger than that in wave equations
[
47
]
. Please see Proposition
7.2
and Proposition
7.1
for details. As for (
1.58
), it is essential for us to use a better wave equations for
\mathbf{v}_{+}
and
\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}h
(see Lemma
2.2
and Lemma
2.3
),

\begin{cases}&{\square}_{g}(\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}h)=D+\frac{1}{\gamma}E,\\
&{\square}_{g}\mathbf{v}_{+}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}{\mathbf{v}}_{-}+\mathbf{Q}.\end{cases}

(1.63)

We shall mention that this good structure of
\mathbf{v}_{+}
is firstly proposed by Wang
[
63
]
.

Operating with
\Delta_{j}
on (
1.63
) and using (
1.59
)-(
1.61
) (taking
r=s-s_{0}+1,k=0
), it follows that

\begin{split}&\|(\Delta_{j}(d\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}dh),\Delta_{j}d\mathbf{v}_{+})\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
\lesssim\ &\|\Delta_{j}D\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-s_{0}+1}}+\|\Delta_{j}E\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-s_{0}+1}}+\|\Delta_{j}\mathbf{Q}\|_{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-s_{0}+1}}\\
&+\|\Delta_{j}(\rho,\mathbf{v},h)\|_{{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-s_{0}+2}}}+\|\Delta_{j}(\mathbf{T}{\mathbf{v}}_{-})\|_{{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-s_{0}+1}}}\\
&+\|[\mathbf{T},\Delta_{j}](\mathbf{T}{\mathbf{v}}_{-})\|_{{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-s_{0}+1}}}+\|[\square_{{g}},\Delta_{j}]{\mathbf{v}}_{+}\|_{{L^{1}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s-s_{0}+1}}}.\end{split}

(1.64)

Multiplying (
1.64
) by
2^{(s_{0}-2)j}
, squaring and summing it over
j\geq 1
, we get

\|(d\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}dh),d{\mathbf{v}}_{+}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{s_{0}-2}_{\infty,2}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{v},\rho,h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|h\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}+}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+}}.

(1.65)

Using (
1.65
) and Sobolev imbedding for
h
and
\mathbf{v}_{-}
, allows us to prove (
1.58
). Moreover, when
2<s\leq\frac{5}{2}
and
a<s-1
, a general Strichartz estimate yields

\begin{split}\|\left<\partial\right>^{a-1}(d\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}dh),\left<\partial\right>^{a-1}d\mathbf{v}_{+}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}\dot{B}^{0}_{\infty,2}}\lesssim&\|\mathbf{v},\rho,h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|h\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}+}}\\
\qquad&+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{2}_{[-2,2]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+}}.\end{split}

(1.66)

The entire proof can be found in Proposition
7.3
and Proposition
7.4
.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 6: Proof of the estimates \eqref{pre6}-\eqref{pre7}}
. Through the above steps, we have obtained sufficient regularity of the characteristic hypersurfaces to control
the characteristic hypersurfaces. This allows us to use the idea of constructing approximate solutions by wave-packets
[
47
,
45
,
64
]
. The whole proof is presented in Section
8
.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 7: Continuous dependence.}
If we prove the continuous dependence directly, we will find that the structure of the system is destroyed and there is loss of derivatives. In section
9
, we overcome the difficulty by using (
1.59
) and a frequency decomposition technique. We first prove convergence in a weaker space. Secondly, we construct a sequence of smooth solutions
\{(\mathbf{v}^{l},\rho^{l},h^{l},\mathbf{w}^{l})\}_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
satisfying (
9.9
)-(
9.12
), which crucially relies on the Strichartr estimates (
1.59
) for Equation (
9.66
). Finally, we use we prove the convergence in
\in H^{s}_{x}\times H^{s}_{x}\times H^{s_{0}+1}_{x}\times H^{s_{0}}_{x}
by making use of
(\mathbf{v}^{l},\rho^{l},h^{l},\mathbf{w}^{l})
. See section
9
for details.

1.5.2.
A sketch of proof of Theorem
1.6

Concerning
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in H^{s^{\prime}},s^{\prime}\leq 2
, to overcome the difficulty in establishing Strichartz estimates, we also treat the large rough initial data by transforming it to small smooth initial data. This can be achieved by a standard frequency truncation, scaling and localization techniques. In this process, we have deal with the fact that the time-interval is smaller and varies with frequency. In the framework of small smooth initial data, so we are able to restore Smith-Tataru’s approach and obtain a sequence of small smooth solutions and its Strichartz estimates. After scaling, we can switch it back to a sequence of large smooth solutions on these smaller time-intervals. By a careful analysis of these solutions of high frequency and the difference of low frequency we obtain uniform Strichartz estimates of solutions of large data on a uniform time interval. Using energy estimates of (
1.1
)-(
1.3
) and its structure, we obtain a strong solution by taking a limit of these smooth large solutions. Our main steps are as follows.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 1: Energy estimates}
. The energy estimate
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{2}}
is established in Theorem
1.6
. Therefore, if we expect a bound of the energy

\begin{split}&\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{2}},\end{split}

then the goal is to estimate

\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho\|_{L_{[0,t]}^{2}L_{x}^{\infty}}.

(1.67)

We refer the reader to Section
3.2
.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 2: Constructing a strong solution as a limit of smooth solutions}
.
Since the initial data is not so smooth, we apply a mollifier to
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
. Thus we consider a sequence

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}_{0j},\rho_{0j})=(P_{\leq j}\mathbf{v}_{0},P_{\leq j}\rho_{0}),\quad\mathbf{w}_{0j}=\textrm{e}^{-\rho_{0j}}\textrm{curl}\mathbf{v}_{0j},

where
P_{j}=\sum_{j^{\prime}\leq j}P_{j^{\prime}}
and
P_{j^{\prime}}
is a LP operator with frequency supports
\{2^{j^{\prime}-3}\leq|\xi|\leq 2^{j^{\prime}+3}\}
.

Considering above initial datum
(\mathbf{v}_{0j},\rho_{0j},\mathbf{w}_{0j})
, there exists
T^{*}>0
,
\bar{M}_{2},\bar{M}_{4}>0
(
T^{*}
,
\bar{M}_{2}
and
\bar{M}_{4}
only depends on
C_{0},c_{0},s
and
M_{*}
) such that a sequence solutions of (
1.18
) satisfy
(\mathbf{v}_{j},\rho_{j})\in C([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{s})\cap C^{1}([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{s-1})
,
\mathbf{w}_{j}\in C([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{2})\cap C^{1}([0,T^{*}];H_{x}^{1})
. Moreover, for all
j\geq 1
,
\mathbf{v}_{j}
and
\rho_{j}
satisfy a uniform Strichartz estimate

\|d\mathbf{v}_{j},d\rho_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\leq\bar{M}_{2}.

(1.68)

Additionaly, for
\frac{s}{2}\leq r\leq 3
, considering

\begin{cases}\square_{{g}_{j}}f_{j}=0,\qquad[0,T^{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
(f_{j},\partial_{t}f_{j})|_{t=0}=(f_{0j},f_{1j}),\end{cases}

(1.69)

where
(f_{0j},f_{1j})=(P_{\leq j}f_{0},P_{\leq j}f_{1})
and
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H_{x}^{r}\times H^{r-1}_{x}
, there is a unique solution
f_{j}
on
[0,T^{*}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that for
a\leq r-\frac{s}{2}
,

\begin{split}&\|\left<\partial\right>^{a-1}d{f}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq{\bar{M}_{4}}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r-1}}),\\
&\|{f}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}{f}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H^{r-1}_{x}}\leq{\bar{M}_{4}}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}).\end{split}

(1.70)

Based on (
1.68
)-(
1.70
), then we are able to obtain a strong solution of Theorem
1.6
as a limit of the sequence
(\mathbf{v}_{j},\rho_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j})
. Please refer Subsection
10.1
for details.

It remains for us to prove (
1.68
)-(
1.70
).

\bullet\ \textit{Step 3: Obtaining a sequence of solutions on small time-intervals}
. Note
\|{\mathbf{w}}_{0j}\|_{H^{2+r}}\lesssim 2^{jr}
(
r\geq 0
). By using compactness, scaling, and physical localization, we shall get a sequence of small initial datum. By using Proposition
10.2
, we can prove the existence of small solutions with these small datum. On one hand, Proposition
10.2
can be derived from (
1.66
). On the other hand, returning back from small solutions to large solutions
(\mathbf{v}_{j},\rho_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j})
, then the time of existence of solutions
(\mathbf{v}_{j},\rho_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j})
depends on
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Because the norm
\|{\mathbf{w}}_{0j}\|_{H^{2+}}
depending on
j
. Moreover, we precisly calculate
T^{*}_{j}=2^{-\delta_{1}j}[E(0)]^{-1}
(
\delta_{1}=\frac{s-2}{10}
and
E(0)=\|\mathbf{v}_{0j}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\rho_{0j}\|_{H^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0j}\|_{H^{2}}
, c.f (
10.4
) and (
10.55
)). On
[0,T^{*}_{j}]
, the solutions
\mathbf{v}_{j}
and
\rho_{j}
yields a higher-order Strichartz estimates,

\|\left<\partial\right>^{a-1}d\rho_{j},\left<\partial\right>^{a-1}d\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}_{j}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C(1+E(0)),\quad a<s-1.

(1.71)

Therefore, by choosing a suitable number
a=9\delta_{1}
, for high frequency
k\geq j
, then we get

\|P_{k}d\rho_{j},P_{k}d\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}_{j}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C(1+E^{3}(0))2^{-\delta_{1}k}2^{-7\delta_{1}j}.

(1.72)

Decompose

\begin{split}d\mathbf{v}_{j}=P_{\geq j}d\mathbf{v}_{j}+\textstyle{\sum}^{j-1}_{k=1}\textstyle{\sum}_{m=k}^{j-1}P_{k}(d\mathbf{v}_{m+1}-d\mathbf{v}_{m})+\textstyle{\sum}^{j-1}_{k=1}P_{k}d\mathbf{v}_{k}.\end{split}

Then we need to see if there is a similar estimates for
P_{k}(d\mathbf{v}_{m+1}-d\mathbf{v}_{m})
. By using a Strichartz estimates for linear wave, for
k<j
, we shall also obtain

\|P_{k}(d\rho_{m+1}-d\rho_{m}),P_{k}(d\mathbf{v}_{m+1}-d\mathbf{v}_{m})\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}_{j}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C(1+E^{3}(0))2^{-\delta_{1}k}2^{-6\delta_{1}j}.

(1.73)

We prove (
1.72
)-(
1.73
) in Subsection
10.3.2
.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 4: Extending a sequence of solutions to a regular time-intervals}
. Compared (
1.71
) with (
1.72
)-(
1.73
), there is a loss of derivatives in (
1.72
)-(
1.73
). The benifit relies on some decay estimates (
1.72
)-(
1.73
) on frequency number
j
and
k
. Inspired by Bahouri-Chemin
[
8
]
, Tataru
[
54
]
and Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
1
]
, if we can always extend it with a length
\approx T_{j}^{*}
of time intervals, and with a number
\approx(T_{j}^{*})^{-1}
, then we shall sum these estimates up and obtain a uniform Strichartz estiamtes
\|d\rho_{j},d\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{L^{1}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}
and
\|d\rho_{j},d\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}
on a regular time-interval
[0,T^{*}]
. In fact, we can extend it in this way from
[0,T^{*}_{j}]
to
[0,T^{*}_{N_{0}}]
. We first extend it from
[0,T^{*}_{j}]
to
[0,T^{*}_{j-1}]
. In this process, the growth in Strichartz estimates and energy estimates can be calculate precisely. Then we conclude it by induction method. Please refer Subsection
10.3.3
for details. Finally, we get

T^{*}=2^{-\delta_{1}N_{0}}[E(0)]^{-1},

where
N_{0}
is an fixed integer depending on
M_{*}
and
C_{0},c_{0},s
(please see (
10.58
)). Furthermore,

\|\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\rho_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{s}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,T^{*}]}H_{x}^{2}},

and

\|d\rho_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|d\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T^{*}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}

is uniformly bounded (See (
10.168
)-(
10.169
)). In a similar idea, (
1.69
)-(
1.70
) also holds. For the whole proof, see Section
10
.

1.5.3.
A sketch of proof of Theorem
1.5
.

The main idea is in the following.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 1: Energy estimates}
. By using the structure of (
1.1
) and a fractional Leibniz rule, the energy estimate
\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}}
is established in Theorem
1.5
. As a result, if we expect a bound of the energy

\|\mathbf{v}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|\rho\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|h\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon}}+\|\mathbf{w}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,t]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}}

then the goal is to estimate

\|d\mathbf{v},d\rho,dh\|_{L_{[0,t]}^{2}L_{x}^{\infty}}.

(1.74)

See Section
3.3
for its proof.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 2: Constructing a strong solution as a limit of smooth solutions}
.
Since the initial data is not so smooth, we apply a mollifier to
(\mathbf{v}_{0},\rho_{0},h_{0},\mathbf{w}_{0})
. Thus we consider a sequence

\displaystyle(\mathbf{v}_{0j},\rho_{0j},h_{0j})=(P_{\leq j}\mathbf{v}_{0},P_{\leq j}\rho_{0},P_{\leq j}h_{0}),\quad\mathbf{w}_{0j}=\textrm{e}^{-\rho_{0j}}\textrm{curl}\mathbf{v}_{0j},

where
P_{j}=\sum_{j^{\prime}\leq j}P_{j^{\prime}}
and
P_{j^{\prime}}
is a LP operator with frequency supports
\{2^{j^{\prime}-3}\leq|\xi|\leq 2^{j^{\prime}+3}\}
.

Considering above initial datum
(\mathbf{v}_{0j},\rho_{0j},h_{0j},\mathbf{w}_{0j})
, there exists
\bar{T}>0
,
\bar{M}_{1},\bar{M}_{3}>0
(
\bar{T}
,
\bar{M}_{1}
and
\bar{M}_{3}
only depends on
\bar{M}_{0}
and
C_{0},c_{0},\epsilon
) such that a sequence solutions of (
1.13
) satisfy
(\mathbf{v}_{j},\rho_{j})\in C([0,\bar{T}];H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}})\cap C^{1}([0,\bar{T}];H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}})
,
h_{j}\in C([0,\bar{T}];H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon})\cap C^{1}([0,\bar{T}];H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon})
,
\mathbf{w}_{j}\in C([0,\bar{T}];H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon})\cap C^{1}([0,\bar{T}];H_{x}^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon})
and

\|d\mathbf{v}_{j},d\rho_{j},dh_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,\bar{T}]}L_{x}^{\infty}}\leq\bar{M}_{1}.

(1.75)

Moreover, for
\frac{7}{6}\leq r<\frac{7}{2}
, consider the following linear wave equation

\begin{cases}\square_{{g}_{j}}f_{j}=0,\qquad[0,\bar{T}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
(f_{j},\partial_{t}f_{j})|_{t=0}=(f_{0j},f_{1j}),\end{cases}

(1.76)

where
(f_{0j},f_{1j})=(P_{\leq j}f_{0},P_{\leq j}f_{1})
and
(f_{0},f_{1})\in H_{x}^{r}\times H^{r-1}_{x}
. Then there is a unique solution
f_{j}
on
[0,\bar{T}]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that for
a\leq r-\frac{7}{6}
,

\begin{split}&\|\left<\partial\right>^{a-1}d{f}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,\bar{T}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq{\bar{M}_{3}}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{{H}_{x}^{r-1}}),\\
&\|{f}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H^{r}_{x}}+\|\partial_{t}{f}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H^{r-1}_{x}}\leq{\bar{M}_{3}}(\|{f}_{0}\|_{H_{x}^{r}}+\|{f}_{1}\|_{H_{x}^{r-1}}).\end{split}

(1.77)

Based on (
1.75
)-(
1.77
), then we are able to obtain a strong solution of Theorem
1.5
as a limit of the sequence
(\mathbf{v}_{j},\rho_{j},h_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j})
. Please see Subsection
11.1
for details.

It remains for us to prove (
1.75
)-(
1.77
).

\bullet\ \textit{Step 3: Obtaining a sequence of solutions on small time-intervals}
. We note
\|{\mathbf{w}}_{0j}\|_{H^{2+\epsilon}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{j}{2}}
, and also
\|{h}_{0j}\|_{H^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{j}{2}}
. By using compactness, scaling, and physical localization, we shall get a sequence of small initial datum. By using Proposition
11.2
, we can prove the existence of small solutions with these small datum. On one hand, Proposition
11.2
can be derived from (
1.66
). On the other hand, returning back from small solutions to large solutions
(\mathbf{v}_{j},\rho_{j},h_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j})
, then the time of existence of solutions
(\mathbf{v}_{j},\rho_{j},h_{j},\mathbf{w}_{j})
depends on
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Because the norm
\|{\mathbf{w}}_{0j}\|_{H^{2+\epsilon}}
depending on
j
. Moreover, we precisly calculate
T_{j}=2^{-\frac{j}{3+\epsilon}}[\mathbb{E}(0)]^{-1}
, where
\mathbb{E}(0)=\|\mathbf{v}_{0j}\|_{H^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|\rho_{0j}\|_{H^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|h_{0j}\|_{H^{\frac{5}{2}}+\epsilon}+\|\mathbf{w}_{0j}\|_{H^{\frac{3}{2}}+\epsilon}
and please see (
11.4
) and (
11.15
) for its formulations. On time-interval
[0,T_{j}]
, the solutions
(\mathbf{v}_{j},\rho_{j},h_{j})
yields a higher-order Strichartz estimates,

\|d\rho_{j},d\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T_{j}]}C^{a}_{x}}\leq C\{T_{j}\}^{-(\frac{1}{2}+a)}(1+\mathbb{E}(0)),\quad a<s-1.

(1.78)

Therefore, for high frequency
k\geq j
, by choosing a suitable number
a=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\epsilon}{40}
in (
1.78
), so we obtain

\begin{split}\|P_{k}d\mathbf{v}_{j},P_{k}d\rho_{j},P_{k}dh_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T_{j}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq&C(1+\mathbb{E}^{3}(0))\cdot 2^{-\frac{\epsilon}{40}k}2^{-\frac{\epsilon}{10}j}2^{-\frac{1}{2(3+\epsilon)}j},\end{split}

(1.79)

Decompose

\begin{split}d\rho_{j}=P_{\geq j}d\rho_{j}+\textstyle{\sum}^{j-1}_{k=1}\textstyle{\sum}_{m=k}^{j-1}P_{k}(d\rho_{m+1}-d\rho_{m})+\textstyle{\sum}^{j-1}_{k=1}P_{k}d\rho_{k}.\end{split}

Then we need to see if there is a similar estimates for
P_{k}(d\mathbf{v}_{m+1}-d\mathbf{v}_{m})
. By using a Strichartz estimates for linear wave, for
k<j
, we shall also obtain

\begin{split}&\|P_{k}(d\rho_{m+1}-d\rho_{m}),P_{k}(d\mathbf{v}_{m+1}-d\mathbf{v}_{m}),P_{k}(dh_{m+1}-dh_{m})\|_{L^{2}_{[0,T_{j}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
\leq&C(1+\mathbb{E}^{2}(0))2^{-\frac{1}{3+\epsilon}m}2^{-\frac{\epsilon}{10}k}2^{-\frac{7\epsilon}{10}m}.\end{split}

(1.80)

We prove (
1.78
)-(
1.80
) in Subsection
11.3.1
.

\bullet\ \textit{Step 4: Extending a sequence of solutions to a regular time-intervals}
. Compared (
1.71
) with (
1.79
)-(
1.80
), there is a loss of derivatives in (
1.79
)-(
1.80
). Fortunately, there are some decay estimates (
1.79
)-(
1.80
) on frequency number
j
and
k
. If we can always extend it with a length
\approx T_{j}
of time intervals, and with a number
\approx(T_{j})^{-1}
, then we shall sum these estimates up and obtain a uniform Strichartz estiamtes
\|d\rho_{j},d\mathbf{v}_{j},dh_{j}\|_{L^{1}_{[0,\bar{T}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}
and
\|d\rho_{j},d\mathbf{v}_{j},dh_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,\bar{T}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}
on a regular time-interval
[0,\bar{T}]
. In fact, we can extend it in this way from
[0,T_{j}]
to
[0,T_{N_{1}}]
. We first extend it from
[0,T_{j}]
to
[0,T_{j-1}]
. In this process, the growth in Strichartz estimates and energy estimates can be calculate precisely. Then we conclude it by induction method. Please refer Subsection
11.3.2
for details. Finally, we get

\bar{T}=2^{-\frac{N_{1}}{3+\epsilon}}[\mathbb{E}(0)]^{-1},

where
N_{1}
is an fixed integer depending on
\bar{M}_{0}
and
C_{0},c_{0},\epsilon
(please see (
11.17
)). Furthermore,

\|\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|\rho_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}}}+\|h_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{5}{2}+\epsilon}}+\|\mathbf{w}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{[0,\bar{T}]}H_{x}^{\frac{3}{2}+\epsilon}},

and

\|d\rho_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,\bar{T}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|d\mathbf{v}_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,\bar{T}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|dh_{j}\|_{L^{2}_{[0,\bar{T}]}L^{\infty}_{x}}

is uniformly bounded (See (
11.123
)-(
11.124
)). In a similar idea, (
1.76
)-(
1.77
) also holds. For the whole proof, see Section
11
.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

Section
2
is concerned with deriving the structure of equations and some commutator estimates and some useful lemmas. This includes the highest-order derivatives of vorticity and entropy, i.e. Lemma
2.4
, Lemma
2.5
, and a wave equation for
\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}h,\mathbf{v}_{+}
in Lemma
2.2
and Lemma
2.3
. In Section
3
, we prove some energy theorems Theorem
3.2
, Theorem
3.5
, and Theorem
3.3
for Theorem
1.4
, Theorem
1.5
and Theorem
1.6
respectively. We also prove the uniqueness of solutions based on these energy estimates, i.e. Corollary
3.6
, Corollary
3.7
, and Corollary
3.8
. In Section
4
-
8
, we give a proof of existence of solutions for Theorem
1.4
. Section
9
presents the proof of continuous dependence of solutions in Theorem
1.4
. In Sections
7
and
8
, we also establish some Strichartz estimates for the acoustic metric. Sections
10
contain the proofs of Theorem
1.6
. Finally, in Section
11
, we prove
Theorem
1.5
by using a similar idea as in the proof of Theorem
1.6
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Reduction to a hyperbolic system

Let us introduce a symmetric hyperbolic system to 3D compressible Euler equations (
1.8
).

Lemma 2.1
.

[
33
]

Let
(\mathbf{v},{\rho},h)
be a solution of (
1.8
). Then it also satisfies the following hyperbolic system

{A}^{0}(\mathbf{U})\partial_{t}\mathbf{U}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}{A}^{i}(\mathbf{U})\partial_{x_{i}}\mathbf{U}=0,

(2.1)

where
\mathbf{U}=(v^{1},v^{2},v^{3},p(\rho),h)^{\mathrm{T}}
and

A_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}&0&0&0&0\\
0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}&0&0&0\\
0&0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}&0&0\\
0&0&0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}&0\\
0&0&0&0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\end{array}\right),\quad A_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v_{1}&0&0&1&0\\
0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v_{1}&0&0&0\\
0&0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v_{1}&0&0\\
1&0&0&\bar{\rho}^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}c^{-2}_{s}v_{1}&0\\
0&0&0&0&v_{1}\end{array}\right),

A_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v_{2}&0&0&0&0\\
0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v_{2}&0&1&0\\
0&0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v_{2}&0&0\\
0&1&0&\bar{\rho}^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}c^{-2}_{s}v_{2}&0\\
0&0&0&0&v_{2}\end{array}\right),\quad A_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v_{3}&0&0&0&0\\
0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v_{3}&0&0&0\\
0&0&\bar{\rho}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}v_{3}&1&0\\
0&0&1&\bar{\rho}^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}c^{-2}_{s}v_{3}&0\\
0&0&0&0&v_{3}\end{array}\right).

Lemma 2.2

(The wave equation for
\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}h
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)
be a solution of (
1.8
). Let
{\rho}
and
\mathbf{w}
are defined in (
1.4
) and (
1.5
). Then
\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}h
satisfies

\square_{g}(\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}h)=D+\frac{1}{\gamma}E.

(2.2)

Here
D
and
E
are defined in (
1.14
).

Proof.

In (
1.13
), we note

\begin{cases}&\square_{g}{\rho}=-\frac{1}{\gamma}c_{s}^{2}\Delta h+D,\\
&\square_{g}h=c_{s}^{2}\Delta h+E.\end{cases}

Therefore we have

\square_{g}(\rho+\frac{1}{\gamma}h)=D+\frac{1}{\gamma}E.

∎

Lemma 2.3

(a wave equation for
\mathbf{v}_{-}
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)
be a solution of (
1.8
). Let
\mathbf{v}_{+}
and
\mathbf{v}_{-}
be described in (
1.11
)-(
1.12
). Then
\mathbf{v}_{+}
satisfies

\begin{split}&\square_{g}v^{i}_{+}=\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}v_{-}^{i}+Q^{i}.\end{split}

(2.3)

Proof.

By Lemma
1.1
, we find that

\square_{g}v^{i}=-\mathrm{e}^{{\rho}}c_{s}^{2}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}+Q^{i}.

Substituting (
1.11
) and (
1.12
) in the above equation, we have

\begin{split}\square_{g}v_{+}^{i}=&-\square_{g}v_{-}^{i}-\mathrm{e}^{{\rho}}c_{s}^{2}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}+Q^{i}\\
=&\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}v_{-}^{i}-c_{s}^{2}\Delta v_{-}^{i}-\mathrm{e}^{{\rho}}c_{s}^{2}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}+Q^{i}\\
=&\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}v_{-}^{i}+c_{s}^{2}\left(-\Delta v_{-}^{i}-\mathrm{e}^{{\rho}}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}\right)+Q^{i}\\
=&\mathbf{T}\mathbf{T}v_{-}^{i}+Q^{i}.\end{split}

∎

Lemma 2.4

(Transport equations for
\mathbf{w}
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{v},\rho,h)
be a solution of (
1.8
). Let
{\rho}
and
\mathbf{w}
are defined in (
1.4
) and (
1.5
). Then
\mathbf{w}=(w^{1},w^{2},w^{3})^{\mathrm{T}}
satisfies

\mathbf{T}w^{i}=(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla)v^{i}+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\epsilon^{iab}\partial_{a}\rho\partial_{b}h.

(2.4)

Furthermore, we have

\mathrm{div}\mathbf{w}=-(\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla){\rho},

(2.5)

and

\begin{split}\mathbf{T}(\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i})=&(\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}\cdot\nabla)v^{i}-\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}-2\epsilon^{imn}\partial_{m}v^{j}\partial_{n}w_{j}+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\partial_{l}\rho\partial^{i}h\\
&-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-2}\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\partial^{i}\rho\partial_{l}h+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\Delta\rho\partial^{i}h+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial^{m}\rho\partial_{m}\partial^{i}h\\
&-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial_{m}\partial^{i}\rho\partial^{m}h-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial^{i}\rho\Delta h.\end{split}

(2.6)

and

\begin{split}&\mathbf{T}(\mathrm{curl}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{i}+F^{i})=\partial^{i}\big(2\partial_{n}v_{a}\partial^{n}w^{a}\big)+K^{i},\end{split}

(2.7)

where

F^{i}=-\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{j}\rho\cdot\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}_{k}-2\partial^{a}{\rho}\partial^{i}w_{a}+2\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{j}v^{m}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}h-\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{k}h\Delta v_{j},

(2.8)

and

\begin{split}K^{i}=\sum^{5}_{a=1}K^{i}_{a},\end{split}

(2.9)

and

\begin{split}K^{i}_{1}=&-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{j}\rho\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\partial_{l}\rho\partial_{k}h-2\epsilon_{kmn}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{m}v^{a}\partial_{j}(\partial^{n}w_{a})\\
&+2\epsilon_{kmn}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{m}v^{a}\partial_{n}w_{a}\partial_{j}{\rho}+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{l}\rho\partial_{k}h\partial_{j}\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\\
&+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\partial_{k}h\partial_{j}\partial_{l}\rho+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\partial_{l}\rho\partial_{j}\partial_{k}h\\
&+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{j}\rho\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\partial_{k}\rho\partial_{l}h-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{k}\rho\partial_{l}h\partial_{j}\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\\
&-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\partial_{l}h\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\rho-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial^{l}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\partial_{k}\rho\partial_{j}\partial_{l}h\\
&+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{j}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-3)\rho})\Delta\rho\partial_{k}h+\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{j}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-3)\rho})\partial^{m}\rho\partial_{m}\partial_{k}h\\
&-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{j}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-3)\rho})\partial^{m}h\partial_{m}\partial_{k}\rho-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{j}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-3)\rho})\partial_{k}\rho\Delta h\\
&-\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial_{k}\rho\partial_{j}\Delta h,\end{split}

(2.10)

\begin{split}K^{i}_{2}=&-\frac{2}{\gamma}\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial_{j}\partial^{m}h\partial_{m}\partial_{k}h\\
&+\frac{2}{\gamma}\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial_{j}(c^{-2}_{s})(c^{2}_{s}\partial^{m}\rho-\frac{1}{\gamma}c^{2}_{s}\partial^{m}h)\partial_{m}\partial_{k}h\\
&-\frac{2}{\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{j}v^{n}\partial_{n}v^{m}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}h+\frac{4}{\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\partial_{a}v^{a}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{n}v^{m}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}h\\
&-\frac{2}{\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{j}v^{m}(\partial_{m}v^{a}\partial_{a}\partial_{k}h-\partial_{a}h\partial_{m}\partial_{k}v^{a}-\partial_{k}v^{a}\partial_{a}\partial_{m}h),\end{split}

(2.11)

\begin{split}K^{i}_{3}=&\frac{1}{\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{a}v^{a}\partial_{k}h\Delta v_{j}+\frac{1}{\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{k}v^{a}\partial_{a}h\Delta v_{j}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \\
&+\frac{1}{\gamma}\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{k}h(\partial^{m}v^{a}\partial_{m}\partial_{a}v_{j}+\Delta v^{a}\partial_{a}v_{j})\\
&+\frac{1}{\gamma}\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial_{k}h\partial^{m}(c^{-2}_{s})\partial_{m}(c^{2}_{s}\partial_{j}\rho-\frac{1}{\gamma}c^{2}_{s}\partial_{j}h)\\
&+\frac{1}{\gamma}\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial_{k}h\Delta(-c^{-2}_{s})(c^{2}_{s}\partial_{j}\rho-\frac{1}{\gamma}c^{2}_{s}\partial_{j}h)\\
&-\frac{1}{\gamma}\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial_{k}h\partial_{j}\Delta h,\end{split}

(2.12)

\begin{split}K^{i}_{4}=&-2\partial^{a}{\rho}\big(\partial^{i}w^{m}\partial_{m}v_{a}+w^{m}\partial^{i}(\partial_{m}v_{a})-\partial^{i}v^{k}\partial_{k}w_{a}\big)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \\
&-2\partial^{a}v^{k}\partial_{k}{\rho}\partial^{i}w_{a}+2\mathrm{e}^{{\rho}}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}^{a}\partial^{i}w_{a}+2\epsilon^{ajk}\mathrm{e}^{{\rho}}w_{k}\partial_{j}{\rho}\partial^{i}w_{a}\\
&+2\mathrm{e}^{-{\rho}}\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}\partial^{a}{\rho}\partial^{i}w_{a}-2\epsilon_{amn}\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-3)\rho}\partial^{a}{\rho}\partial^{m}h\partial^{i}\partial^{n}\rho\\
&-2\epsilon_{amn}\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\partial^{a}{\rho}\partial^{i}(\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho})\partial^{n}\rho\partial^{m}h\\
&-2\epsilon_{amn}\bar{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\mathrm{e}^{h+(\gamma-2)\rho}\partial^{a}{\rho}\partial^{n}\rho\partial^{i}\partial^{m}h,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \end{split}

(2.13)

K^{i}_{5}=2\partial^{i}{\rho}\partial_{j}v^{a}\partial^{j}w_{a}-2\partial_{j}v^{a}\partial^{j}\partial^{i}w_{a}-\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\partial_{j}v^{m}\partial_{m}(\mathrm{e}^{-{\rho}}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}_{k}),\ \ \ \ \

(2.14)

\begin{split}K^{i}_{6}=&2\partial^{i}\rho\partial_{n}v_{a}\partial^{n}w^{a}-\mathrm{e}^{\rho}\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}\cdot\epsilon^{ijk}\partial_{j}(\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\mathrm{curl}\mathbf{w}_{k})+2\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}\cdot\partial^{a}{\rho}\partial^{i}w_{a}\\
&-2\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}\cdot\partial_{j}v^{m}\partial_{m}\partial_{k}h+\mathrm{e}^{-\rho}\epsilon^{ijk}\mathrm{div}\mathbf{v}\cdot\partial_{k}h\Delta v_{j}.\end{split}

(2.15)

Proof.

From (
1.2
), we can calculate
